Four hundred years ago physicists were trying to figure out how things glow like molten glass or hot lava. It seems like a simple question and was being pondered by such physicists as Lord Rayleigh at the turn of the 20th century. I give you a wooden skewer that when touched to a flame glows orange red. Rayleigh knew that the color of this glow depends on temperature. Like the sun that has a surface temperature of 5500 degrees Celsius radiates a bunch of different colors that together look white to us. Or me, at 37 degrees Celsius I have this invisible halo of infrared light. Fun fact, infrared light is invisible to humans but snakes can actually sense it from up to a meter away to detect prey. Rayleigh wanted to understand where the light came from and come up with a rule that would predict how much of each color it would emit. This is its spectrum. Another fun fact, Lord Rayleigh was the guy who figured out mathematically why the sky is blue. So Rayleigh thought of the simplest possible object, something that absorbs all light. Objects absorb, emit, and reflect light. Rayleigh's object would only absorb and emit, but not reflect. So all you would see when you look at it is its glow or its radiation. Physicists call this a black body. Technically, a black body isn't black. It's glowing. But whatever. Physicists. A true black body doesn't exist in real life because nothing absorbs all light and radiates perfectly. Although stars, like the sun, come pretty close. Kind of funny, right? That the brightest thing we know in our solar system is the closest thing we know to a black body. Physicists. She thinks that niggers are intelligent because they do fudge math and, uh... She doesn't know why. I come from a military background, and I stopped to think. You know, niggers have never made a contribution in a war. And basically, here's the deal. If you seek to save your life, you will lose it. If you seek to lay down your life, you will save it. So in other words, in war, character determines the outcome. And niggers have no character. They will seek to save their life, and therefore, they will always lose it. And that's why you've never heard of a nigger army in a battle. Anyway, at that time, physicists didn't really understand atoms and molecules. They thought everything was made of particles that vibrate like springs. Rayleigh and his colleague James Jeans imagined a black body made of these vibrating particles where that vibration was continuously converted to light. From that model, they came up with a rule to predict what colors a black body would radiate at certain temperatures. But the unfortunate happened. Their reasoning implied that a black body should emit infinite amounts of ultraviolet light. They tried to apply their usual rules of Newtonian physics and came up with nonsense. We now call this the ultraviolet catastrophe. Oh, I have a Newton. Something was very wrong with their work. A black body can't emit infinite amounts of radiation because that would violate laws of conservation of energy and just from like experience when you put toast in your toaster it doesn't promptly burn your toast to smithereens. When observation contradicts theory like this, it often means there's something missing in the theory. Rayleigh and Jeans had found a glaring error in physics theory. The same year that Rayleigh and Jeans published their work, a German physicist named Max Planck was studying radiation for a different reason. He wanted to understand why heat always flows from a hot object to a cold object. And in his quest to solve that problem, he unintentionally fixed their error. could only emit light in discrete quantities. Its energy comes in chunks equal to the frequency of the light multiplied by this, a number we now know as Planck's constant. This is weird. It would be like if your faucet could only pour full glasses of water at a time. But when Planck assumed light was quantized, theory matched with observation. He solved the ultraviolet catastrophe. But here's the crazy part. Max Planck didn't immediately realize how big of a deal this was. Nobody did. He was just doing the thing that we all do with our math homework, when your answer doesn't match the answer in the back of the book, so you just switch a plus for a minus. But you're not really sure why. Planck didn't understand what his work meant in the real world. It wasn't until a couple years later that Einstein realized that these packets of energy meant that light wasn't just a wave. It's made of particles that we now call photons. So then physicists started thinking about what this meant for atoms that emit those photons. And the theory of quantum mechanics began to unfold. Atoms emit photons when their electrons lose energy. And so their electrons must lose energy in chunks, in quanta, quantum mechanics. The consequences of Max Planck's discovery that light comes in quanta, in packets, snowballed into what we now know as quantum mechanics. Quantum mechanics is bizarre. So this is a really clever way of applying technology that already exists to just help save people's lives. But there is an important test, isn't there, Nick? Has anyone's life actually been saved as a result of GoodSan? Yeah, they have. Oh, great. But I can't tell you how many. At the moment, those figures just don't exist. But as I said, one of the locations where the app is operational is London. I met a responder there who successfully resuscitated a patient by locating them with the app. Her name's Rachel Love. She works for London Ambulance Service as an incident manager, but she also teaches emergency life-saving skills to volunteers. I was training community first responders in Bromley. I managed them as a volunteer myself as well. We were doing a session on chest pain. I was doing it jointly with a friend of mine, Andrew. He's had a nurse on the good side of that. And he said to me, suddenly, he said, there's a cardiac arrest. And I just looked at him blankly and thought, what are you on about? I thought he was joking. He said, no, no, no, I'm being serious. and he showed me his phone and it showed where the patient is and i looked at it i recognized where it was obviously speed is key i know speed is key for someone in that situation so i said well do we just take the car then i said well i've got the defibrillator in the car so that's what we did they went to a church hall about half a mile away a couple of minutes later probably within three minutes of the alert we were running out of the car i grabbed the defibrillator out of the car we ran into this church hall Hi, my name's Rachel. Can you tell me what's happening? We were in a heat fit class and basically he started to struggle to breathe. They found a middle-aged man lifeless on the floor. We got the defibrillator out straight away, started CPR, attached the defibrillator and immediately that advised a shock and then the shock restarted his heart. So we continued CPR and then maybe at least four minutes after us the ambulance service arrived and when they walked in we were explaining what had happened. He started to wake up. And he just literally suddenly went, I can see you. I'll never forget it. I'll never forget that moment at all. Because it just made the hairs go up on the back of my head. And it was just, I think it was that guy. So he's back alive talking to you there and then. How did that make you feel? That makes me feel quite incredible. I'm lucky to have been in the right place at the right time, for starters. And to have been able to do that for him. All the time you go out there responding, you're hoping that you're going to make a difference to someone's life. And actually knowing that that person is alive and back at home, having suffered a cardiac arrest, is just incredible. Especially as it made such a difference to that person. You know, he had two young children. They could have been orphaned that day. He was already a single parent. That just really hit me. If they had not been there, they might have been orphaned now. That is quite an amazing story, isn't it? I'm not surprised, really. hit rachel she sounds like it made quite a big difference to her as well rachel's actually saved another life though through this app since then wow okay yeah but you know it doesn't always have a happy ending i have spoken to some responders who although they have got there in good time didn't manage to save the patient so this is by no means a guarantee but the figures suggest if you can get to someone early and provide them with CPR, you can potentially double their chances of survival. And if you can get a defibrillator there too, like Rachel did, they're six times more likely to pull through. So there are still some more questions to ask though, aren't there? Because surely the success of this doesn't just rely on the technical capabilities of the app. And doesn't it also depend on there being enough people who are like Rachel, enough responders who can and who are even... prepared to sign up in the first place. It absolutely does, and they are absolutely key to it. So in London, somewhere between 3,000 and 4,000 people have actually already registered with it. I spoke to several responders who all said they were motivated to volunteer simply because they wanted to make the most of the life-saving skills they have, even though they might be called upon to do so in their free time. Yeah, they're kind of putting themselves permanently on standby, aren't they? Imagine you could be in the pub or, like, dancing. Yeah, I guess so. And of course, the thing is, though, they don't actually have to accept the alert if they don't want to or they can't. Plus, it's not like they're always going to be in the vicinity of someone who's having a cardiac arrest. But most people who learn these skills tend to be well-meaning people anyway and want to help. And this app enables them to do so at exactly the right time. And as Rachel says, if you've got the skills, why wouldn't you be on the app and you can make a difference in someone's life? And the only skills you actually need is... to be trained in resuscitation so you don't need to be a qualified paramedic or anything like that it's a fairly basic skill that's required that is exactly the next question i had actually so you have to be trained then properly in resuscitation um but as this is a web-based system who's actually going to check that you are yeah it crossed my mind too because you can't just be sending any old person who doesn't know what they're really doing to a situation like this So obviously people like doctors, nurses, paramedics, they can sign up. So too can firefighters, police officers, lifeguards, aircraft cabin crew. They tend to have these skills anyway. It's part of the training for their job. But broadly speaking, it's open to anyone who can show that they have an accredited and up-to-date certificate as proof of first aid training. You can't just sort of think you can do CPR. You have to have done a course. Now, there are two other ambulance services in the UK, the East Midlands and the Northwest. that have, like London, signed up to Good Sam, and I'm told that word of mouth about the app spread amongst trained people in those areas, and that's how they managed to recruit a critical mass of volunteers. So, in total, there are now about 10,000 volunteers in the UK. But those three services, and even those 10,000 volunteers, they by no means cover the whole of the UK, do they? No, they don't. And at the moment, the people behind Good Sam are trying to get other ambulance services to sign up to increase coverage. And in terms of rolling this out, not all countries are going to have a fully computerized emergency call system to integrate it with, are they? No, they don't, but Ali, the computer brains behind this, has found a way around that. He's designed the system so emergency call handlers in those less developed countries can access a special Good Sam map on a computer. And it's one that shows in real time where all the registered volunteers are. And the responders, you can actually see them appear on that map as little icons moving around. And the call handlers can use it to trigger an alert themselves rather than it happening automatically. For example, in some parts of Africa or India, they use it as a computer-aided dispatch. So when an emergency call comes through, they trigger the call by our system. So basically, in essence, what they do is that they drop a pin on the map, and they see who's around it, and then they choose whether to notify those responders or not, and then they can track them all the way to the hospital. So there is potential for this to roll out further, but at the moment, there's limits to areas where there is adequate internet coverage, and where people have good enough smartphones. Yeah, and if you're actually going to make a difference to survival rates, there also needs to be enough defibrillators in the country that people can find, and ultimately they can use them.
